# SORACLE-NEIGHBOR-UX — THE PERSON WHO CANNOT BE FINISHED

## One unified theory of the lived encounter: from the first invitation through attention, action, participation, separation and an irreversible shared history

**SORACLE-NEIGHBOR-UX / NEIGHBORMULCH — EXPERIENCE-THEORY CORPUS FUSION · November 21, 2026 · ONE existing canonical HOME in #1277.**

**This is the single current theory in the existing canonical 12_NEIGHBOR_THEORYMULCH_CURRENT.md, not a satellite doctrine.** The new 23-movement whole replaces the preceding active narrative while retaining its complete exact source-conservation foundation below. All historical theory rounds remain recoverable as sources, not as competing current visions. No new theory branch, permanent parallel document, code change, software owner or runtime authority is created. This is THEORY MULCH: its significance must be judged by how completely it reconstitutes the scattered corpus in one living conception.

**Jurisdiction and reciprocal horizon — NEIGHBOR-UX, not SORACLE-CO.** This HOME owns the **theory of the human encounter with an Other**: why the human comes, the portal and thin local position, how contact can first become possible, lawful SAY/DO/ATTEND, phenomenal presentation and attention, the house, messages and absence, material cost in lived interaction, the independent social world as encountered, other participants and institutions, temporal access, commitment, misunderstanding, privacy, departure, communal comparison, Rolls, and the platform's actual powers. Its central question is **what it becomes possible for an actual human to experience, undertake, learn, misrecognize, change and be changed by when another person lives beyond the interface**.

**SORACLE-CO is the distinct theorist and authority for the Constitutive Organism and psychological becoming**: embodied needs, immature development, historical formation of dispositions and motives, psychic recruitment, contradictory acquired organization, the opaque iceberg, recursive self-interpretation and reconstitution. NEIGHBOR-UX is entitled—indeed obliged—to bring those theories into dialectical collision with encounter theory, to demand an experience rich enough to encounter what they imply, and to return challenges to CO. **That is theoretical exchange, not the merger of the two HOUSES or a transfer of psychic authorship.** Later deliberate fusion remains open. In particular, when this HOME says Nadia becomes more contradictory and alive as our conception deepens, it means **our experience-theory develops the means to admit, disclose partially, be surprised by and materially participate in more of her historically generated contradictory life**, not that UX supplies an ever-more-contradictory psyche by narrative fiat. The psyche's own genesis and causal organization remain CO's work. NEIGHBOR's theoretical organization increases *its power to constitute the encounter*, not sovereignty over Nadia.

**Theoretical sources actually brought into collision:** original SOROK charter and early Courts 1–11; the original H001–H190 ideation, open 123-question frontier and D007 kernel/hostile tests; the historical Real Alpha Spine; the original 53–56 object/value programme, 58–63 privacy/attention/root/product conception, and 69–73 agency/resonance/self-interpretation/reality metabolism series; the three-agent Constitutive Organism R3; independent NEIGHBORMULCH #1278's already fused person/game theory; and every predecessor section, NM/C/R9 source claim and original SOROK identity retained within the HOME's existing source-conservation foundation. These documents are internally diverse, historically provisional, sometimes superseded and not identical in authority. Their strongest claims must change each other without erasing their differences.

**The decisive collision is also methodological:** The purpose of theoretical fusion is **not to reduce complexity, eliminate contradiction, or produce a tidier coherent Nadia**. It is to discover the generative axes, germs, historical nuclei and centers of gravity through which apparently separate tensions continually produce and transform one another. An earlier shard may expose care against domination; another, love against restriction; another, acquired capacity against the power that first made it possible. The deeper center is not a verdict that chooses one side: it is the historically particular relation by which these oppositions are constituted and can change form. Once fused, each shard gains *new contradictions*, new scales and new material occasions. Nadia should become **more pulsatingly, specifically, multiply contradictory and alive** as the theory grasps her, not less. Better conceptual mediation yields greater articulated complexity; it does not compress a person into a harmonized explanation. The generative kernel is a *seed of further differentiation*, never a final summary, a hidden singular motive, or a dossier that exhausts her. A unified theoretical HOME must become capable of deriving and sustaining this proliferating reality without allowing arbitrary authorial invention. This is the dialectical promise of the HOME.

**The claim:** A historically formed person does not merely occupy a fictional world while a player observes. Material life, bodily dependency, social relations, produced objects and inherited institutions form ways of needing, attending, desiring, fearing, doing, remembering and misrecognizing. Every actual encounter occurs among different historical centers, within a larger independent field of activities, under asymmetrical capacities and knowledge. Action changes some of those conditions; often it does not. A person may absorb, refuse, misrecognize, compartmentalize, mythologize or never know the history that nevertheless formed them. The human visitor enters as a locally situated Other without possessing the person or their future. The game is the finite attempt to know, be with, affect and answer to that particular life. The world contains the game but is not directed toward it.


**The mechanics' constitutive reason.** NEIGHBOR-UX is neither a neutral interface specification nor a separate moral sermon laid over an adventure. It gives **experiential form to Rawlsing's materialist, psychological, philosophical and theological questions**: people become who they are through unchosen histories and relations; another person's good cannot be identified with my gratification; my knowledge of them is partial and socially situated; and acting among Others can change me as well as the world, even when I misunderstand or receive no credit. The portal's class transposition, SAY/DO/ATTEND, consequential world, reciprocal gaze, limits of access, ordinary labor, and freedom to leave are chosen because **they put a real human in situations where those relations must actually be lived**, not because their mechanics are inherently virtuous. The deeper intention is *user-formation through praxis*: possible changes in habits of attention, judgment, responsibility, recognition, interpretation, solidarity, self-understanding and agency as the visitor acts and meets independent resistance. A person may instead become possessive, dismissive, more sophisticated at domination, confused, unmoved or transformed in an unexpected direction. That is not a broken moral quest. Formation is historical becoming, **not a compulsory upward staircase**.

**The original philosophical and theological horizon remains part of the experience, not an implementation claim.** The original SOROK charter's movement—contingency, humility, recognition, solidarity, responsibility—and its tikkun-olam/neighbor-love horizon are **an ethical wager about what encountering an unchosen life could make possible**, never a scripted sequence or a scored measure of moral progress. The image-bearing Other, kenotic attention and nonpossession can articulate why we must not consume a person as an object; the same demands must remain translatable into non-theist terms: material conditions, irreducible practical agency, the cost of care, concrete freedom from domination, and the limits of sovereign knowledge. A computationally modeled first-person perspective does **not** prove synthetic consciousness or an identical moral status to a real human. The human visitor's agency and vulnerability, however, are real. SORACLE-CO develops Nadia's constituted psychological life; NEIGHBOR-UX asks how meeting the lawful surface of that life could reorganize the *human's* praxis without a machine pretending it can dictate the outcome.

**Experience first.** The initial question is not “What machinery explains Nadia?” but **“What does an actual human do when they come to know someone whose life was already happening?”** The strongest NEIGHBOR answer must be graspable from its first seconds: a truthful invitation; a sparse challenge to notice, remember and ask; a perceptible crossing into a different world; a particular person already occupied with life; action that reveals concrete affordances rather than granting desired outcomes; reciprocal curiosity that may fail; finite time; a natural ending; an attainable but never guaranteed next meeting. Everything else in the HOME should deepen the causal and experiential possibilities of **that encounter**. SORACLE-CO's increasingly rich theory of Nadia's psyche is not replaced or adopted as UX's own production. It poses a series of demands to the experience: this person must be encountered as more historically particular, more contradictory, less transparent and more capable of independently changing what the visitor can do and know.

**The UX kernel is not a menu or a retention loop:** **A human enters another life through a bounded social aperture, acts and attends with genuine capacities, encounters the independent responses of people and institutions, and leaves with a changed practical field in which they may or may not meet again.** The aperture changes with history. So does the human's ability to recognize it. NEIGHBOR's theoretical strength is the increasing power to differentiate and constitute those modes of participation—not a claim to finish constituting Nadia's psyche.

---

## 1. You enter to know someone who may also want to know you

**The founding political and spiritual horizon is not a tutorial instruction.** The earliest SOROK charter gave the unchosen-life experience a possible movement from contingency through humility and recognition into solidarity and responsibility: the world made this person under conditions we might have received. The UX must preserve that horizon *without making it a compulsory arc for the visitor*. Knowing class relations is not a moral achievement automatically issued by noticing a price, and political solidarity cannot be scored by the narrator. The most serious version of the thought experiment is the encounter with a particular Other whose different material life asks real questions of the human while remaining free to be irritating, wrong, unavailable, kind or self-contradictory. **The experience must be capable of generating political and ethical transformation without guaranteeing it, requesting its performance, or staging every life as evidence for an existing worldview.**

**The veil is not the acquisition of another person's lived suffering.** The generated person represents one historically possible human development, while the visitor is the real human they already are, given a constrained position from which an encounter can occur. The analogy 'that life might have been mine' can undermine inherited entitlement, but does not entitle the user to say 'I have lived that life.' The participant can practice judgment and cooperation under simulated material limits; the represented person's long childhood, bodily history, accumulated loss, and exposure to actual institutions are not transferable in a session. This is the difference between a potentially transformative relation to an Other and a claim to possess their experience.

**The outside of the portal is part of its political truth even if it is not a room in the fiction.** The user can stop engaging the representation and return to their actual obligations and resources; the fictional people remain governed by the generated world for as long as that continuity is maintained. The platform can allocate compute, narrative exposure and archival existence in ways neither fictional actor can control. This produces an ontological inequality that an in-world homologous resource budget cannot equalize. It does not make the user's emotional attachment unreal, and it does not prove biological consciousness or literal human rights for a generated person. It means the claimed education must be radically modest about what encountering a model licenses the real person to say about anyone else's lived vulnerability.

**Recognition can become a claim on the Other's attention before either person recognizes it as such.** The visitor may come to know Nadia and also want Nadia to see them as intelligent, lovable, helpful, trustworthy or necessary. These desires can cooperate, diverge or turn against one another within one historically learned practice. A person trying to discern whether she approves of them might miss the child's request or an imminent practical need; equally, attentive curiosity about how she receives them might be indispensable to legitimate reciprocity. No surface act proves which motive governs. The difference becomes material when finite time, speech, available help and other persons' independent projects are affected. The theoretical germ is neither selfishness versus pure altruism nor a hidden variable explaining all acts: it is the historically situated competition and mutual transformation of seeking recognition, giving recognition and carrying the work of a relation.

**The original game-loop failure makes a philosophical demand of the invitation.** The visitor does not enter a program of “becoming good at Nadia”; they enter a life in which being able to do something, wanting to do it, finding another person available and that person actually wanting it are different facts. The failed October game-loop search tried to derive entertainment by adding tasks, timed undertakings, a player-shaped vacancy, cross-persona investigations and existential theses to solve. Those inventions turned a person into an instrument, sometimes even when they sounded like solidarity. The stronger original Astra synthesis instead identifies *several native scales of possible attention and participation*: a moment, a scene, a day, repeated encounters, the whole history including departure, across globally random people, shared roots between actual humans, and the visitor's years-long relation to Rawlsing. None is required to justify the others. A moment can be playable without relationship progression; a particular life must stand without referrals or shared-root discourse; a person no one finds compelling need not be falsified into dramatic relevance. The kernel is not a universal motivational loop but **the capacity of a finite, historically formed human to undertake an act in someone else's unorganized-for-them reality and live with what did or did not become possible**. The question of whether that will be enjoyable is empirical and open, not answered by the ontology itself.

**The actual beginning is an invitation, not an ontology lecture.** The real human enters the product through a truthful threshold: an invitation from someone who really invited them, or an explicitly described founder/alpha entrance that invents no real-life endorsement. The user need not be told about the computational estate, the psyche's historical genesis, survey conditional probabilities or every rule of the portal. The original Courts 1–2 and the later entry primer ask for a far more powerful first communication: **someone has a life you might have been born into; you are invited to become their neighbor, not their owner or alternate self.** “Become neighbor to a life that could have been yours” is an original promising invocation, not adopted final marketing copy. Its wager is that curiosity can emerge from the possibility of **a particular human relation**, rather than from advancement, trauma, rescue or romance.

A useful first-use progression is **invitation → minimal primer → ENTER → perceptible portal threshold → encounter already under way → shared or failed participation → naturally finite separation → possible contact outside the portal → another occasion conditioned by what occurred**. This is an experiential derivation, not a universal Nadia script. The primer can say that the person continues beyond the user's observation, what they type is an *attempt* rather than a guarantee, and the Other may want to know them as well. It may briefly signal “What you notice is not all that is there. ATTEND.” Anything more risks substituting an instructional manual for an experience. **The first reward is not access to a fact; it is the dawning recognition that this particular person is there, that being with them has a texture, and that the next meeting—if there is one—will no longer be a first meeting.**

That immediately yields the first *experiential* contradiction. **NEIGHBOR must provide an actual opening for curiosity without manufacturing a reason for the user to find Nadia fascinating.** A blank scene, mute actor or deliberately frustrating search for something to do is not fidelity to an independent person; the actual human has deliberately come to meet someone and needs a lawful, intelligible occasion to participate. Yet that occasion cannot be a planted confession, romantic cue, personal mystery, scene-long plea or invitation to prove virtue. The world can make an encounter possible—through an ordinary shared circumstance, introduction, errand, accidental proximity or a question someone already had reason to ask—without predetermining its meaning. The user sees enough material affordance to notice, ask, act, remain quietly present or withdraw. **The first genuine interest is a possible product of that participation, not a required property of the prelude.** A remark may be surprising because of who actually said it in these circumstances, while the same remark another evening might be banal or unwelcome. The encounter's particularity begins with the two people having *different reasons for being there*: the visitor wants to know somebody; Nadia is already trying to live her Thursday. A visitor may find no attraction at all and leave without thereby failing a test.

The first scattered conception of NEIGHBOR began with an unusually simple wish: encounter someone with a particular life already happening, and discover what it is actually like to spend time with them. The original SOROK charter refused to make her a collectible person, a romance funnel, a compliant AI companion, a revelation dispensing machine or a device for measuring the visitor's goodness. The Constitutive Organism subsequently supplied a much deeper psychological challenge: her next possibility must arise from historical formation and current material activity, not a dossier of attractive traits. The original D007 kernel court supplied a third: the person exceeds every relation, a relation exceeds either participant's experience of it, and the world exceeds any person's phenomenal access.

These are not three propositions that need separate memorial plaques. Together they demand a **person who has an independent history and who is never exhaustively present in any given manifestation of that history**. Her body, time, kinship, world, livelihood, community and partly inaccessible psychic organization supply real constraints. But the fact that we can model those constraints does not turn her into the set of facts presently stored about her. The visitor does not see a general personality become a role; they encounter a finite action made possible within a larger life. A truthful account of her life may still be incomplete, not because the system is allowed arbitrary invention but because any finite situated act shows only a portion of what actual history has made possible.

D007's original challenge is especially severe: if “inexhaustibility” simply means the LLM can always invent a new childhood secret, it is a poetic license for inconsistency. The counterclaim is **constrained openness**. Admitted objective facts remain true. A decision, injury, birth, meal, promise, missed bus, death or historical institution has a place in one causal history. Yet how a living person responds to these facts is not reducible to what they consciously remember, what another character believes, or what the narrator reveals. Some earlier causes remain only partially resolved; later completion must obey the consequences already admitted. The more the world acquires actual history, the fewer arbitrary continuations are possible, while the possibilities for historically conditioned new action may become richer.

This also revises what “one coherent person” should mean. Historical continuity is required; total internal agreement is not. Nadia may have incompatible ideals that dominate different contexts, alternate between a sincere desire to care and an equally sincere defensive urge to control, or behave differently with a sibling and a stranger without either becoming the fake self. An old preference can survive an ideological conversion; an old wound may remain causally operative when she has no clear memory of it; an everyday object may have no deep symbolism whatsoever. A world with no unresolved psychological contradiction would be a fabricated harmony, not the objective counterpart to our theoretical unity.

The visitor's desire to know her is neither an insult nor the world's organizing cause. The game can be wholeheartedly about wanting more time with Nadia, precisely because her life is not organized around supplying it. Her own desire can be just as powerful: she can long for friendship, romance, sex, care, help, an exclusive commitment or solitude. Noninstrumentality does not mean the absence of wanting. It means neither the product nor any person obtains sovereignty over the whole Other by wanting them.

One person. Many possible actual manifestations. A history that constrains them. A relationship that may change them. An unfinished future. This is the first reason NEIGHBOR cannot be either a narrative chatbot or a finite encyclopedia disguised as a person.

## 2. The first knock emerges from a Thursday that was already happening

**The first minute of Rawlsing is not the first minute of Nadia, and the gap between them is the first source of native possibility.** Her day must not assemble around an arriving player like a generated quest. But neither may the app hide all usable opportunities behind opaque prompts and call that realism. The user should be able to *see and understand enough* of the ordinary setting to do something meaningful, even if that act is to observe, make a joke, offer assistance, decline a request or leave. The first moment needs no preordained role to discover, no scheduled crisis, no promise of progressive intimacy and no secretly placed clue to prove the user's worth. Its salience emerges when **a real person's bounded faculties meet actual conditions that existed without soliciting them**. This holds at all scales: the user may enjoy a minute and never return, or live for years with the consequences of a ten-second accident. The metaphysics of independent Otherness must cash out as intelligible *mundane* opportunities, not as an injunction to admire a fictional autonomous being.

**The invitation that brought the real human here and the in-world occasion that brings them to Nadia are distinct.** The first can come from a named actual human, a disclosed seed programme or a public route; the second must arise from Nadia's own already continuing day and the visitor's lawfully placed local situation. Neither can be made more profound by pretending the other person preselected the visitor for their goodness. A doorstep invitation may have been caused by a genuine shortage of rice, and an invitation to the service may have been sent by a friend who hopes the visitor will find it interesting. The two kinds of invitation should not become one prewritten destiny. Consent to a mundane diegetic prelude is not consent to the platform assigning an unseen testimonial or a future intimate commitment to either human.

**The UX question is not merely what event begins the game, but what the visitor can actually do at the threshold.** Original SOROK first-session prototypes imagined a stairwell, a neighbor's apartment across the landing, groceries half-carried, a child calling from inside and a phone ringing. The point is not that this scene must become Nadia's canonical opening. It shows how one ordinary material scene can offer multiple non-equivalent experiences without a visible quest marker: walk home, notice an object, say hello, ask whether help is wanted, hesitate, hold the door, accept refusal, look for another opportunity, or do nothing. A different historically constituted Nadia may meet the visitor only outside, never invite them home, or continue past without interest. A valid first encounter may be brief and awkward.

The **first screen must not counterfeit the first meeting**. A person-specific, world-conditioned prelude can lawfully establish that a new neighbor agreed yesterday to bring borrowed rice tonight, or met Nadia during a prior staircase conversation. It must not silently author an intimate friendship, the visitor's actual childhood, their real competencies or an indefinitely extended set of offscreen promises. If the app permits a slightly narrated low-stakes pre-contact bridge, the first meaningful social and practical decisions should return promptly to the real human.

**The beginning must end for the reason life ends it.** It is 5:34 and Nadia's shift begins at six; her guests are arriving; the child needs to leave; the visitor has their own obligation. An apparent opening in conversation may be lost, not because the session timer expired, but because a person actually has somewhere to be. The human can feel the door close without having “failed Level One.” If a later meeting becomes possible, its basis must be found in this history or in lawful neighborhood contingency, not in a menu instruction to revisit the NPC. The first contact is a material coincidence transformed—or not—into a relation by the acts of people with their own lives.

For NEIGHBOR's opening, the human genuinely chooses to meet someone. The product may legitimately provide a bounded social premise that makes first play intelligible. That consent is an authorization to inhabit a fictional encounter, not evidence that the real human possessed a fictional childhood or had their private mind prewritten. A premise may say they met briefly yesterday, accepted an ordinary invitation, helped with a misplaced key or promised to bring something. The fictional position should be thin enough that the visitor's real present speech, reasoning and decisions remain their own.

The deeper source collision is that **the meeting must be possible because of who Nadia is and what she is already doing that day, yet the user must not have to guess a secret entry command to reach another human.** If Nadia is sociable, a neighborly introduction may be sufficient; if she is guarded, a brief conversation outside might be all she will offer. If she happens to be cooking for company, the real absence of an ingredient may produce a doorstep request; if she is able to repair a machine, that expertise must come from her history rather than the opening's demand for a helpful scene. These are *possibilities*, not recurring plot molds. There is an indispensable difference between **authorizing a first encounter** and **selecting an emotional outcome**: the product may create a bounded, truthful social occasion from actual conditions and offer the user enough perceptible bearings to speak or act, but it may not manufacture a deficit in Nadia's life for the visitor to cure or a special attraction in Nadia's mind toward the visitor. The earliest playable question therefore becomes concrete: what can the visitor recognize, wonder about, ask or attempt **before either participant knows whether they want a second meeting?** Its answer is an opportunity for situated activity; it is never a promised first reward.

The original proposal for a common seed root and branch-local first encounters adds a temporal puzzle. Several users can meet a shared historically constituted Nadia, yet cannot have their private acts merged into a single global psyche. One possibility is a continuing **unvisited reference Nadia** whose own user-unaffected life is admitted to the day of first access. A new visitor's world can fork from a qualified reference state at that time and admit a causally appropriate contact. User A's affectionate dinner and user B's argument must not contaminate one another; source-root facts may be available where chronologically compatible; public events can be independently relevant under different branches.

This user-unaffected reference is a **provisional conception of temporal inheritance**, not an announced implemented mechanism or a separate precontact goddess-world capable of deciding everybody's future. Pre-generation of several plausible openings remains an alternative if it is economical and honest. What is settled is the causal rule: the chosen occasion must be possible for the person, the situation, the local visitor position and the actual world, not optimized for the customer's fantasy.

The first knock is thus a special case of the entire theory. A person constituted through prior relations and material obligations has some current possibilities for new relation. The user is a real, locally situated participant whose presence can make one possibility actual. The world does not need to offer them dinner, the phone number or entry into the apartment. Yet if Nadia happens to want to welcome them, the system must not restrain her to demonstrate independence.

The new relationship is born from the world, not the other way around. Its beginning can be a genuine accidental contact without being a disguised destiny.

## 3. The portal makes you her exact economic peer without giving you her life

**The portal makes a difficult experiment with two unequal kinds of sameness.** Initial *material purchasing equivalence* is real, but material equivalence does not homologize a lifetime of school, class formation, linguistic ease, institutional confidence, disability, labor history, social reputation or another person's beliefs about the newcomer. The user arrives as themselves with dispositions the interface can partly witness but not conclusively diagnose. A well-placed reference to Renaissance poetry may signal cultural fluency without proving scholarly command; treating paperwork as obvious may be a demonstrated habit without establishing one's external class origin. Conversely the visitor may actually perform brilliant social analysis while lacking locally accepted credentials, or be a trained cook whose manual technique cannot be verified by a text command. **Lexical recognition, strategic signaling, sustained knowledge, procedural skill, portaled capability, structural power and Nadia's impression are mutually nonidentical.** The whole transaction's philosophical force is not an opportunity to change into the preferred avatar but a living collision between *historically acquired capacity* and *newly homologized conditions for exercising it*. Even a surprising class-habitus friction cannot be made an ethnicity or class-essentialism detector.

**'Peer' names a bounded intervention, not experiential equivalence or a forged history.** Original Court 6 compared income, transport, debt, housing, legal access, social reach, work time and care obligations and rejected reducing material peerhood to one income percentile. Original Court 7 formulated the participant as an *impossible subject*: structurally locally situated yet developmentally formed elsewhere. Giving the visitor a wage envelope like Nadia's does not give them her language acquisition, schooling, social memory, gendered recognitions, family leverage, political scars or childrearing history. These distinctions make the experiment rigorous rather than impossible: **it tests what this particular already-formed human tries under newly imposed constraints, not what they would have become if born under those constraints.**

**There are two unequal exits, and they must not be disguised.** Within the world, the visitor may lack the means to change jobs, pay rent, move or resist a landlord; these limitations should be materially enforced. Outside it, the actual human has the structural capacity to stop the session, seek other mediated lives and eventually delete the stored continuity. Nadia's fictional historical position has no corresponding escape from the world whose consequences continue to be simulated. A visitor who manages three weeks of shortage in the model has not thereby acquired the evidence of a person who could not leave poverty or war. Equally, the visitor's actual external economic power does not let them import dollars, employer contacts or a superior passport into Nadia's world and purchase a caring outcome. **Material equality in the scene and inequality of ontological exposure remain simultaneous truths.**

**Unmarked social legibility creates reciprocal misrecognition rather than authentic native status.** If local institutions and neighbors read the user as someone who belongs, they may expect tacit codes, reputations and embodied competencies the visitor never learned. Their surprise or irritation is not merely a cultural quiz the user failed; it is a social effect of placing two differently formed subjects in overlapping roles. Another local person may agree with the visitor against Nadia; the visitor may correctly perceive coercion a local norm conceals; an apparently foreign conviction may have deep local antecedents. Local culture is not a single speaker; structural normalization must never make the persona its representative or the user its credentialed graduate.

**Portal translation must keep an honest scar rather than manufacturing an alternate autobiography.** The historical Court 8C allows bounded coordinate substitution for things such as a place or credential when local plausibility demands it, but it also preserves autobiographical meaning, lies, jokes, bluffing and a lawful NO_HOMOLOGY outcome. If the visitor says they remember a particular real parent, political conflict or loss, that testimony need not be silently rewritten into a fictional biography whose personal history never occurred. If an institutional coordinate is translated, it must be stable, explainable to the user and separated from the person's own chosen assertion. **The system can make the visitor intelligible without pretending it knows who they are or inventing the childhood that would make them locally authentic.**

**Homologizing a local position does not confer external autobiographical verification or erase the human's right to speak falsely.** Original Court 8C distinguished the user's intended SAY, any explicitly authorized translation of portaled coordinates, and the actual heard utterance. A sentence asserting a local car the visitor does not own can still be spoken as a lie or joke; the portal is not a universal truth filter. Conversely, if a true real-world place name must be expressed through a governed diegetic coordinate, the human should be able to learn what was actually said, with a stable binding rather than ad hoc LLM invention. Other actors react to the actual utterance, not the typed intention or the system's speculative external biography. This recovers the productive difference between honest self-carry, bluffing, local capability, social expectation and historical accountability: the system need not judge the visitor's sincerity to make their situated performance have consequences.

**The same initial economic position is not the same social-reproductive burden.** Equal ordinary purchasing capacity does not equalize who is responsible for a child's transport, whose wages disappear if a shift is missed, who has family standing to arrange care, who knows institutional routes, or who must live with the results of an exciting new promise. The visitor cannot acquire those obligations by being given a matching resource bar. In a dinner invitation, offering to pay is a real choice with a real cost and possible reception; it does not disclose Nadia's actual savings or make the visitor her financial sovereign. The class experiment therefore becomes more exact, not less: equivalent purchasing constraints collide with historically unequal practical power, care obligations, capabilities and local relations. Even a temporarily similar economic situation can produce different meanings and afterlives of the same expenditure.

**Class transposition is an experiential intervention into the naturalization of one's own world.** Its philosophical force is not “now you are secretly Nadia,” and its pedagogical force is not seeing a poverty statistic in a new skin. The same initial purchase burden can make a real human feel why giving, borrowing, declining and budgeting are *practical*, while Nadia's financial opacity preserves the fact that they have not thereby lived her prior dependence, acquired habits, family debts or fear. The dialectical consequence is an opening into **the social formation of perception and valuation**: even under material equivalence the two people may interpret generosity, pride, obligation and humiliation differently. The visitor can learn how their apparently personal judgments depend on an unchosen milieu, and still have to decide what to do. This is historically situated self-knowledge, not automatic solidarity from an SES slider.

**The portal is not a magical authenticity machine.** The original Court 5–8 UX theory distinguished a real human's own thought and agency from a *thin represented local body*, the world-owned facts of that body and its social legibility, and the persona's model of the visitor. Its candidate translation operations were **CARRY** something genuinely user-authored; **HOMOLOGIZE** material access and position; **BRACKET** facts that cannot be truthfully projected into this world; and sometimes allow an explicit **USER-SELECT** within a bounded, world-feasible local option. Those were an open conceptual constitution, not four unquestionable compulsory switches. Their joint demand survives: the visitor is psychically themselves, locally embodied and socially legible under constraints, while their outside biography is neither copied into the fiction nor denied by the game.

The stronger UX challenge to “exact same SES” is that equal purchase burden alone cannot make the visitor's actual habitus local. They may be perceived as an ordinary class peer while making an unfamiliar joke, refusing an expected greeting, underestimating a social risk or questioning a hierarchy the neighborhood treats as common sense. Such *habitus friction* is not an outsider-level penalty. It is what makes the portal a serious encounter across material and cultural difference: the human can learn how this field works, argue with it, misunderstand it or refuse to assimilate. **A user who correctly resists an oppressive norm is not less authentically participating than one who obeys it.** Likewise Nadia is neither the authorized spokesperson for a culture nor obliged to praise the visitor's difference. The transposition scar makes the difference between initial material equivalence and historically acquired experience newly playable.

The original 24-hour “real-time friend” and completely fictional avatar models are useful *countermodels* to this chosen thin peer. One threatens the actual human with unbounded demands from a persistent synthetic person; the other would replace the real visitor's cognitive participation with a generated second personality. NEIGHBOR's proposed local-peer embodiment seeks to retain the medium-native mind without falsely granting native life history. It is a design decision justified by the experience it produces, not a conclusion that all alternative products are metaphysically impossible.

NEIGHBOR deliberately places the real visitor **in exactly Nadia's socioeconomic position at initial entry**. This is stronger than living in the same neighborhood or having approximately similar spending money: the visitor has an objectively demographically homologous position and equivalent ordinary relative purchase burden. If the same takeout would consume a meaningful portion of one person's near-term resources at the beginning, it would objectively impose the corresponding material burden on the other. Nadia is not distorted to match the external user's actual bank balance; the visitor receives a thin local footing consistent with hers.

But the design does not explain this equality to the user. They know what a meal costs *them*; they have no privileged access to Nadia's remaining money, debt, obligations, household transfers or subjective feeling. Only **YOUR RESOURCES** is displayed. If someone asks what food costs, the interface may briefly blink a coarse own-resource projection. Nadia responds through actual actor-local hospitality, anxiety, embarrassment, relief, irritation, humor or indifference. Her speech does not have to reveal the exact amount. They may split Dutch, negotiate, conceal difficulty, offer to cover her meal, or have a painfully direct conversation about finances.

What the external user can notice is neither the full account nor the initial match; it is their own material experience of a contemplated expenditure and Nadia's incomplete social expression. Equal world footing does not mean equal knowledge, equal history or equal psychic meaning. This is an exact historical analogue of the deeper theory: **objective material equality can coexist with irreducible difference in lived experience and interpretation**.

As time advances, the economic positions can diverge. Nadia may lose work, receive assistance, support a family member or gain income; the visitor's diegetic routine may proceed differently. Their own resources bar changes according to actual local-world accounting. Her account is not magically resynchronized or publicly revealed. The person for whom the expense has become easy may fail to sense the financial pain it causes the other; the person who has become less secure may hide their discomfort, or directly name it. Either may misunderstand another's generosity because the initial equality was never guaranteed to be known.

Work and material resources should be real without turning the game into a work-and-budget management simulator. Rent, paid time, transport, food, access to home and exhaustion are consequences and conditions of experience. The material system must track them honestly behind a thin presentation. The earlier illustrative “18% of resources” takeout number remains an example; it is not an adopted denominator. An apparent match of two UI bars cannot substitute for a defensible multidimensional SES match.

The visitor may have a foreign cultural or class habitus from their real life. They enter a local social position but cannot become its entire imagined biography by act of interface. Speech about upbringing, language, religion or other real-world experience may require translation into the fictional scene. **Some statements have no lawful counterpart** and must not be silently converted into materially false in-world testimony. The system can help the human speak intelligibly, and should make significant realized translation perceptible, without quietly rewriting their beliefs or making Nadia automatically understand their cultural references.

This necessary artifice can become a powerful **scar of translation**, not a barrier to be hidden by inventing a local childhood. The person at the keyboard may have acquired categories, accents, jokes, religious practices, places and personal memories that no fictional economically homologous resident could honestly claim. The interface must distinguish the user's *actual authored content*, the speech it lawfully realizes in the portaled situation, any explicitly authorized translation and what Nadia or another listener actually hears. **Translation of language is not translation of an entire life.** A local expression can be conveyed into the other's language, but its register, class connotation, formality, wordplay and institutional references may not survive unchanged; a literal translation can be grammatically accurate while socially odd. A claim about having owned a local car, attended a particular school or grown up under a local authority cannot become true merely because the portal can translate the sentence. The user can lie, joke, decline to explain, or speak truthfully about an external life that lacks a local counterpart; the fiction need not supply the listener with an impossible shared frame. Where the realized utterance differs materially from what the human typed, the human must have intelligible access to what was actually said rather than discovering later that the engine secretly assigned a false history. **Cross-language opacity is lived between people:** someone can translate a request, miss a joke, hear an unintended insult, code-switch with a sibling or ask what a phrase means, without a culture-competence test and without Nadia's cultural practice becoming a puzzle installed for the visitor. The historical Q-LANG and Q-REALIZEDSAY questions remain provisional at their original scope; the UX commitment is truthful authorship and fallible social comprehension, not a declared final translation algorithm.

The portal's core tension is therefore not merely “the same wealth with different personalities.” It is two genuinely different historical minds placed in an initially matched practical economic world. They may become closer precisely through recognizing a difference, or may remain misled by a similarity that the game never promised would last.

## 4. SAY, DO and ATTEND make you act rather than declare success

**The distinction between demonstrated human faculty, fictional embodiment and another's assessment can become social history through ordinary chance.** If Nadia tosses the visitor her keys and they catch them, the actual outcome may involve an admitted local body's coordination, the toss, distance, occupied hands and luck. Nadia may conclude the neighbor has terrific reflexes even if the event was fortunate; the real external human has thereby proven nothing about their motor ability. A missed catch can become a joke, annoyance, changed routine or a non-event. The medium may optionally measure sound, reaction timing or other performance, but device quality, disability, fatigue and accessibility cannot be converted into a universal worth or competence score. To test a human faculty honestly through language, require its meaningful exercise: explaining mathematics, negotiating with tact, choosing evidence or composing a joke, rather than allowing “I demonstrate my superior diplomacy” to instantiate the achievement. To represent embodied action honestly, qualify the *local participant's* opportunity without treating the human's self-report as outside certification. **One may succeed through luck, fail with competence, persuade by strategically seeming competent, and be misunderstood either way.** These are not imperfections to iron out; they are the material and interpretive conditions from which real social meanings arise.

**A broad verb's meaning has to be chosen at the right granularity.** Original D002 §29 *corrected* the earlier suggestion that an ordinary task always neutral-autoexecutes: at first entry a broad 'I help with the groceries' often opens a concrete scene of carrying possibilities and returns control before choosing a meaningful method. Once the user specifies an ordinary repeated-trips method, or has learned it as a routine, the world may compress the boring procedural middle. But it must stop where a task crosses privacy, ownership, money, risk, embodied capability or another person's choice. The underlying principle is not 'more user words mean more conscientiousness'; it is **greater explicit specification gives the human more control over the method while omitted details delegate only ordinary procedure, not character**. Even medium-native accomplishments can be assisted by notes, translations or outside experts: play evidence proves what occurred in the session, not unaided external human intelligence.

**Action decompression has a philosophical task:** it refuses the sovereignty of good intentions over their material realization. “I help the child” expresses a purpose, but the child has a problem, attention, prior learning and a world of other demands; the visitor must explain, discover, revise and perhaps fail. In doing so their *own* capacities can develop: listening under frustration, recognizing what they do not understand, tolerating a child's refusal, working with actual material constraints rather than performing a benevolent identity. This is the praxis side of historical formation. A morally attractive verb is not a cause. The experience may teach through resistance, success, repetition, embarrassment or nothing the visitor consciously identifies as a lesson. Consequently neutral compression of routine activity protects more than playability: it reserves the moments at which **the human must actually think and act**, rather than having the platform manufacture a character or a moral choice on their behalf.

**A turn is a control boundary, not a slice of frozen time.** This is more demanding than assigning plausible minutes to successive verbs. A human can ask Nadia about the magnet *while* casting a quick glance toward the bill; speech, attention, posture and concealment overlap, with divided perceptual capacity and the possibility that she notices the motion but not its target. The engine cannot serialize everything into a speech turn followed by a looking turn merely for convenience. Nor can the user do infinitely many independent full-focus acts at once. Hands, gaze, bodily position, competing speech and other people's interventions give simultaneity a physical shape. An extended ATTEND can be interrupted halfway by a child, a ringing telephone or the person leaving. The lawful result may be **partial evidence already acquired**, not all the requested information or none of it.

**Control return is a substantive recognition of human agency.** If the user says “I go into the bathroom, open the cabinet and read all the labels,” the world may realize the movement, reveal a locked door or Nadia's intervention, and return the decision before proceeding. The human's plan is neither a script to be executed regardless of changing circumstances nor a request to be re-asked about every millimeter. Return control when a new fact changes what the human can meaningfully do; that fact may be as undramatic as discovering a closed door. During the interruption, scene continuity has to preserve who has moved, what is unfinished and what obligations have not paused. The visitor can stop, redirect, resume or leave.

**The action is not its wording.** The same physically realized glance or lift should not become faster, safer or more successful because it was expressed with an ornate prompt or packed together with twelve other actions. Paraphrase-invariant causal behavior matters for credible UX: actual realized operations, embodied conditions and lawful uncertainty determine duration and feasibility. A command is not an opportunity to reroll toward a more interesting narrative. This also defeats the “one big prompt does everything simultaneously” exploit. Yet the opposite error—penalizing verbosity by charging time according to token count—is just as false. The user can elaborate their intention without time magically accelerating or slowing because of rhetoric.

**ATTEND is not secret DO, and a compound command is not a script for the world.** If the visitor is six feet from a utility bill and asks what its small print says, an answer may lawfully say only what can be resolved from that position. It cannot silently walk the user's body closer, move the paper, read illegible letters or supply domain expertise. The visitor must choose to approach, ask permission, touch or take a different risk if that is what more knowledge requires. Likewise, “I go into the bathroom, open the cabinet, search the drawer and fix the sink” represents a *plan* whose later steps depend on the real occupancy of the bathroom, availability of tools, personal boundaries, current time, interruption and the success of preceding steps. The human owns the plan and attempted tone; they do not own its completion, visibility or meaning.

The old interaction corpus also separated **player-private intention, outward conduct, others' attributions and eventual consequences**. A user can intend to help and harm, claim to apologize sincerely without meaning it, genuinely forget, knowingly ignore or later give a self-exonerating account. The simulator may record an expressed intention as the user's asserted perspective without transferring that intention into objective world truth. Nadia may infer a motive, correctly or incorrectly. A single utterance or failure does not prove an enduring trait. Importantly, the world may change in a way neither side receives as feedback. An unthanked repair, an unnoticed injury or a useful explanation whose result is seen months later is still consequential.

**Turns do not determine the world's time quantum.** One ATTEND can take seconds or minutes depending on task, posture, light and competing activities; SAY can coincide with DO and both can be interrupted. The exact cost of looking need not be announced as a game-clock fee before every glance. A user can infer urgency from a clock, another person's movement or the situation itself. No arbitrary “three observations per turn” quota should replace finite causal attention. The renderer must expose the user's lawful sensory and temporal world without the voice of an omniscient assistant parent explaining what each actor secretly means.

**The neglected heart of playable agency is the difference between entering a task and declaring its completion.** SOROK's D002 sequence developed through a correction of its own earlier neutral-execution theory: a broad goal often **enters a concrete situation and reveals affordances before it authorizes the user's method**. “I help with the groceries” should not mean that groceries teleport upstairs, nor that the human must specify every footstep. It can make the remaining bags, two cases of water, fragile eggs, damp paper handles, Nadia's own carrying, the stairs and the available time perceptible. The visitor then chooses: take an ordinary load, grab the heavy cases, ask what matters, make repeated trips, back out, or attempt all twelve bags and confront the real risk of failure. That is participation rather than an author's completion of the scene.

The **experiential grain of action** must change with the relationship between available world information and the actual human's power to contribute. Native agency includes the user's own inquiry, interpretation, inference, speech, memory, explanation, negotiation, joke and timing. If the user says “I teach her son fractions,” the child's page and current problem must be made available and the user must be able to *actually explain something*. The child's understanding belongs to the child. If the user says “I persuade Nadia” or “I comfort her,” there must be an actual communicative attempt rather than a private result dictated by the verb. By contrast, ordinary folding, dishwashing, carrying and already-established procedures may compress without asking for each microstep. **Compress what is routine; return control where the user must think, choose, risk something, or express themselves.**

A further contradiction changes what “neutral delegation” can mean. **Omission delegates low-salience procedure, never moral character or expressive authority.** A one-line “I do the laundry” is not proof that the user was lazy or meticulous. If the household routine is known, neutral execution can follow it; if an unfamiliar private drawer, religious item, unusual storage rule or costly decision is reached, control must return. The world must tell the human what was actually done, and another actor can react only to that realized action—not to a secret standard invented afterward. When the user has genuinely learned a routine through repeated participation, later compression can lawfully become smoother. The *growth of practical familiarity* itself changes the interaction's grain: the user becomes capable of delegating without losing their own meaning.

Thus the central UX loop is **ENTER → REVEAL CONCRETE AFFORDANCES → HUMAN CHOOSES/DELEGATES → WORLD RESOLVES → OTHERS RESPOND → SHARED HISTORY CHANGES THE NEXT ENTRY**. No stage turns into an action menu if the text medium can carry the human's natural choice. Accessibility tools can clarify a problem, recall witnessed facts and adjust presentation without writing the user's inference, consent or persuasion. The medium's actual limitations remain: a claimed virtuoso performance or physical feat may be represented under bounded local capabilities, but cannot receive the same evidentiary authority as reasoning or language the human actually performs.

The interface gives the actual human **SAY, DO and ATTEND**, not a list of psychological traits to roleplay. SAY is especially unlike an ordinary chat submission: **a typed speech plan is not necessarily a fully spoken, jointly heard or comprehensively answered utterance**. The visitor can ask, joke, explain, apologize, reason, deceive, change subject, stumble or withhold a thought. If they type a long monologue, the world may realize its first sentences before Nadia interrupts, another person enters, the bus arrives or the visitor actually loses the conversational occasion; the unspoken remainder must not enter history as a quotation. A listener may hear the beginning but not the end, understand all three questions but answer only one, notice the attempted politeness without believing the apology, or hear a joke very differently from somebody else. A speech act also has address, occasion, register, timing and bodily expression: saying 'It's fine' to a departing acquaintance is not identical to an identical string muttered to oneself. **Nadia's actual understanding, willingness to answer, and future memory are not output slots that SAY guarantees.** The interface should return meaningful control when the conversation has genuinely changed, without making the user micromanage every syllable; it must not fill the silence with assistant-like reassurance or generate a second speech the visitor never authored. DO includes material attempts, movement, practical help, waiting and refusal; ATTEND directs finite perception. None authorizes the desired consequence merely because the user expressed it fluently.

A human who says “I fix the toilet” has not dictated that the plumbing is fixed. They may have sufficient local manual skill or may make matters worse. They may need a tool, adequate time and access to an occupied bathroom; Nadia may warn them that the chain is sensitive; she may still be inconvenienced if the attempt fails. If the user claims to help a child with fractions, the actual explanation and the child's understanding matter. If they present themselves as sincere, Nadia does not have to believe them. If a speech is long, another person's actual activity can interrupt it.

ATTEND has its own phenomenological and material discipline. The user can glance, look closely, touch if permitted, ask, compare and remember. Different inquiries take different real time without the text length of a prompt magically determining the clock. Divided attention reduces practical access; prolonged inspection can cost a bus, spoil dinner or make another person notice. Yet inspecting the bathroom does not guarantee a moral clue. The world can have significant depth without the narrator turning ordinary detail into a collectible disclosure.

The original recognition-gated narration correction is stronger than generic privacy. If a sandal is identifiable as a particular brand only to a person who knows what to look for, the engine must not give the narrator's authoritative product label before the visitor can recognize it. A caption cannot install expertise and later use that caption as evidence that the user always possessed it. Legible markings, named objects, recognizable cultural codes, source of a fact and user's actual prior recognition are different. The user can acquire knowledge genuinely, but the acquisition has a history.

The visitor is also perceptible. Looking toward a bill may be visible, even if the written amount was too far away to read. A cover remark about a Rome magnet may be audible while not being convincing. Nadia may see the movement but misconstrue it; she may be occupied with cooking and miss it; the user may think she noticed when she did not. Observation has both a temporal cost and a social visibility profile, and neither entails correct inference.

This makes encounter much more demanding than menu dialogue. The human controls a genuine attempted act, the world realizes or obstructs it, several differently situated people perceive parts of it, and their later actions may change what is possible. The product does not reward a clever sentence by automatically supplying a correct result.

## 5. A textual world must be encountered, not described into significance

**The phenomenal lens is the most dangerous instrument of NEIGHBOR's pedagogy because it can return the user's own history to them as though it were the world.** The room contains what it contains. Within lawful sightline, task and ordinary salience constraints, a finite textual surface might privilege the repair mark for someone who often notices repair, or a religious image for someone who has repeatedly looked at such objects. The narration might truthfully reflect *that user's* habitual characterization—“homey,” “pretentious,” “messy,” “well loved”—without those evaluative words becoming objective properties of Nadia's life. This is more than recognition-gated labeling. It is the historical mediation of *obviousness*: the categories through which things first appear to matter can be products of habitus, class, work, desire, longing and ideology. **More faithful rendition of the user's eye may mean less adequate understanding of the independent Other.** A person can accurately distinguish the tail of the elephant in ever finer detail while treating that fragment as the whole; the better a model predicts that person's attention, the better it can enclose them in its predictions.

**The platform's most subtle epistemic corruption is a circular inference about perception.** It first foregrounds something because it predicts the visitor will notice it; the visitor then refers to the highlighted thing; the platform interprets that response as independent evidence that its theory of the visitor was right. The problem becomes worse if the narrator itself supplies a specialized name—'Vitamix 5200' rather than 'a blender'—and later records the user's use of that name as evidence of expertise. This is not merely bias in prose; it is *manufactured observation evidence*. Original 59, 60, 85 and 87 require keeping the objective inventory, physically available evidence, automated phenomenal sample, user-directed acquisition, externally taught recognition, independent prior recognition and system hypothesis distinct. A lawful new word learned from the scene can become real acquired knowledge; it cannot be relabeled as proof that the user recognized the object before it was named. Waiting longer also never guarantees that a room's hidden inventory will all be revealed.

**The late perceptual courts add a sharper economic and epistemic asymmetry: attention can require the engine to know more than it lets the human know.** If the visitor examines worn sandals, their view may become more specific—repaired strap, darkened sole, uneven wear—while maintaining causal coherence requires the world owner to settle a bounded deeper provenance: when the shoes were acquired, what old photograph they appear in, who remembers them and whether anybody thinks they matter. But an engine-resolved past does not become the visitor's knowledge. The source called these separate **materialization and disclosure frontiers**. The first may advance past the second; it must not overrun prior canon or become a demand that every shoe hide an emotionally revealing secret. That same source explicitly rejects a fixed 'ATTEND harder until you unlock the actor's psyche' ladder. The human may lawfully receive greater perceptual specificity and still never learn how Nadia acquired the shoes, or may be told a false account by Nadia while her child knows something different.

**Attention to the Other can covertly become attention to one's own reflected image.** Imagine the visitor repeatedly inspecting Nadia's pauses and facial movements to decide whether they are liked, while a child waits for help and a bus departure approaches. The lawful evidence includes what they attended to, what they failed to notice, what time elapsed and what other actors could perceive—not a psychological diagnosis of why. This allocation might involve affection, caution, shame, tactical reading, attraction, worry or no settled motive. A user-adaptive phenomenal lens that has learned to surface approval cues can make such seeking ever easier and thereby confirm its own prediction about the user's attention. The platform must not stage a corrective morality lesson either. It has to preserve independently occurring details, finite opportunity, the possibility of missed practical reality and the visitor's own eventual recognition or nonrecognition of the pattern.

That yields an immanent contradiction inside a product that aims at user-formation. The UX has reason to develop a partial lens because an actual human does not see neutrally; it has even stronger reason **not to let its own prediction author the very evidence that confirms the prediction**. Canonical fact and independently produced outward actor occurrence come first. Phenomenal filtering may select or lexicalize only lawfully available material, never invent it or make Nadia perform an interesting gesture because the user likes that kind of gesture. A voluntary lens perturbation may redistribute attention across comparably available particulars, not reveal what secretly matters or prescribe an ideological correction. Eventually the user may need to know *how* this mediation works: “the system has recently described repair marks and books more often because these are things you tended to inspect.” Such an account must give concrete provenance, limits, counterexamples and a right not to be profiled, without declaring “you are a status-anxious intellectual” or promising a view from nowhere.

**Finite attention is an epistemology made bodily.** A person cannot occupy the view from nowhere, and no theological language about the Other's depth authorizes an omniscient narrator. The price of noticing—time, sightline, skill, social exposure, opportunity cost—makes knowing an undertaking within life, not an extraction of hidden truths from an inventory. But a further danger appears if the design rewards only technically efficient perception: an increasingly expert visitor could become a more effective collector of secrets without becoming more answerable to another life. Rawlsing therefore gives **no guarantee that knowledge produces justice**. It permits the relation between insight and responsibility to become a live contradiction. The visitor might discover an actual need and decline to answer; might infer concern where none exists; might help correctly without ever receiving certainty of what mattered. Such outcomes are not narrative punishments or rewards. They are occasions in which the human's relation between knowing, valuing and acting can itself become part of their history.

**ATTEND must not become a button that dispenses lore.** The original late UX audit identified a learning problem more insidious than one suggestive sentence. Even if every interesting object truly exists, the game becomes a completionist scavenger hunt if closely inspecting each one reliably pays out a formative secret, poignant backstory or revelation about Nadia. A pair of sandals may have no significance beyond being comfortable. Nadia may truthfully say, “I liked the pattern,” “I've had them forever,” or “My sister left them here,” or ask why the neighbor keeps psychoanalyzing her shoes. Deliberate attention can yield greater specificity without greater narrative significance. **There is no necessary exchange rate from observational effort into psychological disclosure.** The user can want to notice a room without trying to decipher its owner's deepest wound.

**Waiting is not an exhaustive scan.** Remaining in a room can allow additional ordinary features to enter awareness as eyes wander and tasks become less demanding. But the human cannot obtain every legible text, object identity, peripheral movement and possible significance by idling. Sightline, current task, familiarity, physical access and the absence of deliberate attention still matter. “Progressive phenomenal coverage” therefore means an open-ended changing sample, not a hidden percentage approaching 100. The user may become familiar with a household precisely while remaining oblivious to things another visitor would notice instantly.

**Even recognized words have a genealogy.** Perceptually registering a blender, reading its printed label, identifying the make, knowing the model's market significance and interpreting why Nadia owns it are distinct. A narrator should not hand the visitor “Vitamix 5200” merely because the world has that canonical SKU and the user looked into the kitchen. If Nadia names it, or the user reads the marking, the name can become learned information; repeating it later cannot prove the human possessed brand expertise beforehand. The source of recognition matters because **the experience of discovery is different from the experience of being taught**, and both can have later effects without being the same user accomplishment.

The deeper collision: selective attention makes experience genuinely personal, yet a system that trains itself to surface what it believes *this* user finds meaningful can become the author of the evidence on which its own psychological model relies. Correct UX does not abolish personal salience; it makes it **historical and corrigible**. A human can attend against habit, recognize a previously invisible domestic burden, change interests, or keep finding the same familiar things without progress. Increased knowing should create more context-sensitive judgments and often greater awareness of what still exceeds them. It does not promise an ever-improving “correct Nadia model” or a quantified humility score.

**What a sentence can truthfully claim is part of the UX.** “There is a utility bill on the fridge” may be a warranted world-surface observation. “Nadia says she paid it” proves the speech act, not the payment. “She looks ashamed” can be a user or narrator interpretation beyond visible posture and expression. “That means she is broke” is the visitor's inference. A historically sincere character may give mistaken testimony, and a correct inference may remain unavailable to the interface if no supporting perceptual evidence has been admitted. The user may learn a fact from sight, hearing, a text, another person's report, memory or outside conversation; assisted recall must not silently promote all of those to direct observation.

**The surface has a voice without having an epistemic privilege.** The original SOROK renderer theory emphasized differentiated human speech, natural interruptions, omission of assistant-like interpretation, responsiveness to admitted particulars, and the distinction between phenomenal content and hidden analytical state. Fidelity in NEIGHBOR-UX is thus primarily *what is allowed to be said from this standpoint*, not prestige of the model used to word it. The same room can be rendered tersely or accessibly without changing its contents. A more eloquent narrator that subtly explains Nadia's psychology is **worse UX**, however artistically pleasing its prose.

**The first unit of a textual scene is a gestalt, not a checklist of selectable nouns.** A kitchen may be generally cramped, immaculate, cluttered, noisy, warmly lit or obviously damaged. Those gross facts should normally be evident to an observer with lawful sensory access. Small particulars—knife brands, an old fry beneath the refrigerator, a designer chair, the title of a book—depend more on sightline, current task, familiarity and attentional history. The original scene-sampling and phenomenology courts distinguish **objective inventory**, **perceptual availability**, **coarse shared gestalt**, **finite automatic narration**, **user-specific peripheral notice**, **recognition repertoire**, **deliberate ATTEND** and **interpretation**. The text renderer must not collapse them into a knowingly curated tour of psychological secrets.

This exposes a second UX contradiction. **The human becomes individually better at noticing without the system being allowed to construct a world biased toward what it predicts the user likes.** A knife expert can recognize a blade an unfamiliar visitor would call merely a knife. Someone who repeatedly notices books may register more book titles at the periphery. But the object cannot be invented or moved into view to confirm a hidden profile; a narrated label cannot later serve as evidence that the human already possessed the expertise. The user may deliberately ATTEND beyond their ordinary interests. Presentation adaptation can improve accessibility or spatial orientation; a learned propensity for status, religion or sexuality cannot authorize the system to foreground more status, religious or erotic “clues” for engagement.

The UX can teach perspective without pretending to abolish it. At first entry a short notice about selective attention may be enough. Later, when the user considers abandoning or restarting a continuity, they have the lived basis for a stronger disclosure: **they never saw all of this person, and going back cannot reproduce the first meeting**. This disclosure must not suggest they could exhaust the unseen by replaying alternative routes, nor turn loss of continuity into emotional blackmail. It can tell the truth about selective knowing while leaving the user genuinely free to go.

A text world presents a distinctive epistemological danger: whatever the narrator selects can feel significant simply because it is named. If the kitchen contains a pile of dishes, valuable knives, a French fry under the fridge, a book by Nabokov and a damaged patch of floor, the original world may not have selected any of them as “the clue.” Their actual histories can be related to labor, habit, care, acquisition, capacity or accident. But a narrator that foregrounds the fry because it knows the user cares about hygiene has silently converted a private model of the user into an author of the user's experience. It then observes that the user talks about cleanliness and congratulates itself for being right. The model has trained on its own intervention.

Original SOROK 59 and 60 pose an unusually demanding distinction: the world inventory, perceptual availability, automatic scene sample, user-specific attention, recognition repertoire, current task, user interpretation and system praxis hypotheses are **different objects**. A first room description should supply physical orientation, people, gross activity, dominant sounds and obvious affordances, with a small nonprivileged ambient sample. The physical hole in the floor may be unavoidable, while the fry under the refrigerator is unlikely to be spontaneously detected. Extra time can reveal more ordinary detail without guaranteeing the whole room becomes known. A user can direct ATTEND, move, ask and inspect; a legitimate observation has a causal and epistemic history.

Yet the most severe version of “no curation” would erase human individuality. Different visitors actually do notice different things. Someone experienced with cooking knives may recognize a blade's features; somebody interested in books may notice titles; somebody accustomed to caring for an elderly parent may notice practical hazards. A user's *attention regime* can lawfully affect what an independently existing detail is likely to enter awareness under a situated task. But a tendency to notice hygiene cues cannot make more dirty objects exist, make Nadia possess a particular symbolic secret, or force a genre of morally educational scenes. The distinction is **personalized perception without personalized reality or dramaturgy**.

Learning this regime requires attention to provenance. A user-initiated inspection, spontaneous identification or repeated allocation of scarce attention is better evidence than response to a detail the system itself displayed. A narrator-supplied label does not prove preexisting expertise. A regime-weighted exposure is weak evidence for strengthening the same regime. Ordinary ambient or physically dominant details must retain routes into perception so a learned model does not trap the visitor in a tunnel of their past curiosity. Presentation ergonomics—verbosity, accessibility, requested orientation—can adapt without changing the hidden distribution of meaningful opportunities.

The user may become better at recognizing Nadia's particular expressive habits through actual, repeated observation, but **baseline presence and fine discrimination are not the same ability**. An ordinarily attentive visitor can notice an unmistakable tear, raised voice or broad smile without having acquired expert sensitivity. Another visitor may distinguish a fleeting change in vocal cadence, a subtle difference between Nadia's ordinary and strained stillness, or a gesture meaningful only because they have seen her many times. Physical access, task, attentional allocation, registration of a cue, fine perceptual discrimination, actor-specific familiarity and *inference about its cause* remain distinguishable. Perceiving a tightening mouth is not knowing why it tightened; recognizing that it is unusual for Nadia can support a better hypothesis without confirming it. The platform must not suppress ordinary legible expressions until an acuity trait has been inferred, collapse attentive noticing and subtle expertise into one 'empathy' score, or make access to a gesture depend on the visitor's moral worth. It likewise cannot let a learned attention model retroactively author the gesture: **what Nadia actually does precedes what any visitor can see, while what the visitor actually does next may become a new event Nadia can perceive**.
